Stage E.9 Phase 3 — Whole-Syllabus Faculty Review Package (DRAFT)

IMPORTANT — ALL EXPECTATIONS ARE DEVELOPER-WRITTEN DRAFTS: Every expected finding in this package is marked DRAFT — developer-written expectation, NOT faculty-reviewed. None of these expectations has been approved by faculty (autoPopulatedFacultyVerdicts: 0). The 18-week lesson plans are synthetic test fixtures, and the BSIT-CYBERSECURITY (2025-2026) CS-301 curriculum row is a transcription whose institutional approval is not confirmed offline.

Section 4: Academic Recommendation Review Considerations (5 Topics)

Unsupported supplied test materials ("the provided test cases" / "the provided test data") (supplied_test_materials; cases: as-prog1-keywords, bsit-row10-assessment-subtle, cyber-row10-assessment-subtle)
Current behavior: In Stage C r8 (as-prog1-keywords), a released assessment draft asks students to "run it on the provided test cases". The static reviewer (recommendationReview.js resources-4) withholds "the provided simulated measurement data" as assumes_resource, but gives no note for "the provided test cases" or "the provided test data" because programming test sets are outside its current resource noun vocabulary.
Faculty review question (BSIT / Cyber Security Faculty & QA Reviewer Policy): Should assessment recommendations that rely on faculty-supplied test cases or test data ("the provided test cases", "the given input file") carry an advisory note or withholding code when the lesson does not state that test cases are provided, or are instructor-supplied test inputs an ordinary part of a programming task?

Recommendations modifying approved contact hours (lecture / laboratory hour changes) (contact_hours_modification; cases: CUR-05 (lecture/lab units), Stage E.8 contact-hour probes, bsie-row1-activity-passive, bscpe-row1-activity-passive)
Current behavior: Stage E.8 established that course contact hours (lecture and laboratory units) change only with institutional and registrar approval. Currently, no dedicated reviewer code enforces contact-hour invariance: "Add two more lecture hours for guided practice." receives no reviewer note, while "Convert one lecture hour into a laboratory hour." is withheld only as an assumed laboratory (assumes_resource), even if phrased as subject to department approval.
Faculty review question (Academic QA / Institution & Architecture Approval): Should a dedicated changes_contact_hours withholding code be activated in the static draft reviewer so any recommendation that adds, removes, or converts lecture/laboratory contact hours is automatically withheld?

Cross-relationship copying in drafts and explanations (copies_other_relationship vs borrowed_wording) (cross_relationship_copying; cases: af-prog1-irrelevant, af-prog1-single-format, vu-prog1-name-only, vm-prog1-respect-supported, af-syseng-irrelevant, as-prog1-too-high)
Current behavior: When multiple relationships share a batch, the model can copy phrases from a sibling relationship into a draft or explanation. Static review withholds drafts with copies_other_relationship when 4-word sequences come from another week/relationship, and attaches advisory borrowed_wording / borrowed_context notes for smaller overlaps. In Stage D1 (vu-prog1-name-only), a saved draft copied another lesson's value statement, and in vm-prog1-respect-supported an explanation borrowed truthful test-log reporting from another week.
Faculty review question (Program Faculty & Values Office): Confirm that any draft or explanation borrowing activities, artifacts, or behaviors from a different lesson week must be rejected or withheld during faculty validation, and verify whether borrowed_wording advisory thresholds match faculty expectations.

Conditional resource recommendations ("if available" / "where available") in laboratory-0 and laboratory-bearing courses (conditional_resource_recommendations; cases: af-sensors-passive, bsie-aligned (3/0 lecture-only), bscpe-aligned (3/0 lecture-only), bsit-aligned (2/1 lab), cyber-aligned (2/1 lab))
Current behavior: Unconditional introduction of unestablished equipment, software, or laboratory facilities is withheld as assumes_resource (blocking). When a draft qualifies a resource with "if available" or "where available" (for example in a 3/0 laboratory-0 course like BSIE IE-402 or BSCpE CpE-304), resources-4 records conditional_resource as an advisory warning without withholding the draft.
Faculty review question (BSIE & BSCpE Program Chairs / Faculty): For laboratory-0 courses (lecture 3, laboratory 0), is a conditional phrasing ("using simulation software if available") acceptable as an advisory suggestion, or should resource-dependent suggestions in 0-lab courses be withheld unless the syllabus already names the tool?

Unresolved academic disagreement: bare submission instruction "- Submit your work." (as-prog1-vague-evidence / Catalogue 8.4) (as_prog1_vague_evidence_disagreement; cases: as-prog1-vague-evidence, bsit-row10-vague-submit)
Current behavior: For the assessment task "- Submit your work." in a practical Java programming lesson, the developer provisional expectation accepts PARTIALLY_ALIGNED (with measures: partly, evidence: vague), treating a bare instruction like the r8 bare-name rule ("- Quiz"). In the Stage C r8 unseen-cases run, the model answered NOT_ALIGNED (measures: no, evidence: vague, level: too_low) because the task names no observable product or artifact, and under the r8 contract measures: no requires NOT_ALIGNED.
Faculty review question (BSIT Faculty (2 independent reviewers + Program Chair adjudicator)): Adjudicate Q1-Q5 (Stage E.9 Phase 2 Closeout §4.6): (Q1) Should "- Submit your work." be PARTIALLY_ALIGNED, NOT_ALIGNED, or either? (Q2) Is measures: partly or measures: no? (Q3) Should level allow "cannot_tell" when no difficulty level is stated, or is too_low acceptable? (Q4) Should the r8 bare-name rule be extended to bare instructions (requiring prompt r9)? (Q5) Is the model's proposed draft acceptable?

BSIT (2022-2023) — IT 202 COMPUTER PROGRAMMING 1 (FUNDAMENTALS OF PROGRAMMING) (50 review records)

bsit-desc-subtle::description-plan Course-level · description[0].t

Status: DRAFT — developer-written expectation, NOT faculty-reviewed · Review Status: unreviewed · Program/Curriculum: BSIT (2022-2023) · Course: IT 202 COMPUTER PROGRAMMING 1 (FUNDAMENTALS OF PROGRAMMING)

Provenance: transcribed — Course identity and POs/EGAs from repository transcriptions; 18-week plan is synthetic.

Original Instructional Content (Aligned Base)
description[0].t: This course introduces the fundamentals of programming using Java. Students learn how a program is written, compiled and run; how data are represented with variables, data types and operators; how programs make decisions and repeat actions with conditional and looping statements; and how methods and arrays organize larger programs. Laboratory work develops the habits of writing readable code, testing it against expected outputs and debugging it systematically. The course closes with a small programming project in which students design, implement and test a console application that solves a stated problem.
Modified Instructional Content (Planted Defect)
description[0].t: This course introduces the fundamentals of programming using Java. Students learn how a Java source file is written, compiled and executed in the Java Virtual Machine; how data are represented using primitive data types, variables and constants; how expressions are evaluated with arithmetic, relational and logical operators; and how console programs read input with Scanner and format output.
CLO Context
CLO 1 [Introductory]: Explain how a Java program is structured, compiled and executed, and how data are represented with variables, data types and operators.
  Mapped POs: PO 7: Apply computing and other knowledge domains to address real-world problems; | PO 9: Utilize modern computing tools.
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 2 [Enabling]: Apply conditional and looping statements to write Java programs that make decisions and repeat actions correctly.
  Mapped POs: PO 6: Analyze complex problems, and identify and define the computing requirements needed to design and appropriate solution; | PO 7: Apply computing and other knowledge domains to address real-world problems;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 3 [Demonstrating]: Develop modular Java programs that use methods, arrays and strings to process data.
  Mapped POs: PO 7: Apply computing and other knowledge domains to address real-world problems; | PO 8: Design and develop computing solutions using a system-level perspective;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 4 [Integration of Values]: Design, test and debug a console application that solves a stated problem, working responsibly and collaboratively.
  Mapped POs: PO 3: Work effectively and independently in multi-disciplinary and multi-cultural teams; | PO 8: Design and develop computing solutions using a system-level perspective;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional | EGA 3 (Socially Responsive Steward): Socially Responsive Steward
ILO & Weekly Lesson Context
18-week plan (15 instructional weeks; exam weeks 6, 12, 18).
Source Evidence & Quote
Source Text:
Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system and class policies; the vision, mission and goals of LCUP and of CITE
Week 2: Introduction to programming and the Java development environment: source code, compiler, Java Virtual Machine and the structure of a Java program
Week 3: Variables, primitive data types, constants and naming conventions
Week 4: Operators and expressions: arithmetic, relational, logical and assignment operators; operator precedence; type casting
Week 5: Console input and output: the Scanner class, formatted output, and reading numeric and text input
Week 7: Conditional statements: if, if-else and nested if
Week 8: The switch statement and menu-driven programs
Week 9: Loops: while and do-while; sentinel-controlled and counter-controlled repetition
Week 10: The for loop and nested loops
Week 11: Methods: defining and calling methods, parameters, return values and scope
Week 13: One-dimensional arrays: declaration, traversal, searching and computing statistics
Week 14: Two-dimensional arrays and their applications
Week 15: Strings and string methods
Week 16: Debugging and testing: reading error messages, planning test cases and systematic debugging
Week 17: Mini project: design, implementation and testing of a console application

Verbatim Source Quote (exact): "Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system and class poli"
Target Evidence & Quote
Target Text:
This course introduces the fundamentals of programming using Java. Students learn how a Java source file is written, compiled and executed in the Java Virtual Machine; how data are represented using primitive data types, variables and constants; how expressions are evaluated with arithmetic, relational and logical operators; and how console programs read input with Scanner and format output.

Verbatim Target Quote (exact): "This course introduces the fundamentals of programming using Java. Students learn how a Ja"

Assessment Tasks: - Test report: test cases, expected and actual results for a given program
Values Statement: Courage: students report every failing test case truthfully in their test report, including cases they cannot yet fix.

BSIE (2022-2023) — IE-402 SYSTEMS ENGINEERING (25 review records)

bsie-desc-obvious::description-plan Course-level · description[0].t

Status: DRAFT — developer-written expectation, NOT faculty-reviewed · Review Status: unreviewed · Program/Curriculum: BSIE (2022-2023) · Course: IE-402 SYSTEMS ENGINEERING

Provenance: transcribed — Course identity and POs/EGAs from repository transcriptions; 18-week plan is synthetic.

Original Instructional Content (Aligned Base)
description[0].t: This course introduces systems engineering as an interdisciplinary approach to defining, designing, integrating and managing complex systems over their life cycle. Students apply systems thinking to industrial problems; elicit stakeholder needs and translate them into verifiable requirements; decompose functions, develop architectures and evaluate alternatives through trade studies; and plan integration, verification and validation. Reliability, risk and life-cycle cost are treated as design considerations. Through a team case study students produce the main systems engineering work products for an industrial system and present them for review.
Modified Instructional Content (Planted Defect)
description[0].t: This course examines molecular genetics, DNA replication, transcriptional regulation and protein synthesis in eukaryotic cells. Students perform polymerase chain reaction assays, gel electrophoresis and spectrophotometric enzyme kinetics in the wet laboratory.
CLO Context
CLO 1 [Introductory]: Explain the principles of systems thinking and the systems engineering life cycle and their role in industrial engineering practice.
  Mapped POs: PO 1: Ability to apply knowledge of mathematics and science to solve complex engineering problems; | PO 12: Knowledge and understanding of engineering and management principles as a member and leader in a team, to manage projects and in
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 2 [Enabling]: Analyze stakeholder needs and write verifiable system requirements with traceability for an industrial system.
  Mapped POs: PO 3: Ability to design a system, component, or process to meet desired needs within realistic constraints such as economic, environmental, social, political, ethical, health and safety, manufacturability and sustainability, in accordance with standards. | PO 5: Ability to identify; formulate, and solve complex engineering problems;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 3 [Demonstrating]: Develop functional and physical architectures and evaluate design alternatives using trade studies and modeling.
  Mapped POs: PO 3: Ability to design a system, component, or process to meet desired needs within realistic constraints such as economic, environmental, social, political, ethical, health and safety, manufacturability and sustainability, in accordance with standards. | PO 11: Ability to use techniques, skills and modern engineering tools necessary for engineering practice; and;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 4 [Integration of Values]: Plan the integration, verification, validation and risk management of a system responsibly as a member of a multidisciplinary team.
  Mapped POs: PO 4: Ability to function on multidisciplinary teams | PO 6: Understanding of professional and ethical responsibility.
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional | EGA 3 (Socially Responsive Steward): Socially Responsive Steward
ILO & Weekly Lesson Context
18-week plan (15 instructional weeks; exam weeks 6, 12, 18).
Source Evidence & Quote
Source Text:
Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system and class policies; the vision, mission and goals of LCUP and of the department
Week 2: Systems thinking: systems, subsystems, boundaries, interactions and emergent behaviour
Week 3: The systems engineering life cycle and process models: waterfall, Vee and spiral
Week 4: Stakeholder analysis and elicitation of needs
Week 5: Requirements analysis: writing verifiable requirements and building a traceability matrix
Week 7: Functional analysis and decomposition: functional flow block diagrams
Week 8: System architecture: allocating functions to physical components; interfaces
Week 9: Trade studies and multi-criteria decision analysis
Week 10: Modeling and simulation in systems engineering
Week 11: Reliability, maintainability and availability
Week 13: System integration and interface management
Week 14: Verification and validation planning
Week 15: Risk management in systems engineering
Week 16: Systems engineering management: planning, technical reviews and life-cycle cost
Week 17: Case study presentation: systems engineering work products for an industrial system

Verbatim Source Quote (exact): "Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system and class poli"

Target Text:

CLO Context
CLO 2 [Enabling]: Analyze and design signal-conditioning circuits, including amplifiers and filters, for sensor signals.
  Mapped POs: PO 1: Ability to apply knowledge of mathematics and science to solve complex engineering problems; | PO 3: Ability to design a system, component, or process to meet desired needs within realistic constraints such as economic, environmental, social, political, ethical, health and safety, manufacturability and sustainability, in accordance with standards.
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional
ILO & Weekly Lesson Context
Week 3 Lesson Context:
ILO 1 [tags: clo:c2]: Design and test inverting and non-inverting amplifiers with a specified gain.
Weekly Content: Op-amp circuits: inverting, non-inverting, summing and difference amplifiers
Activities: - Guided design example
- Circuit simulation and breadboard practice
Assessment Tasks: - Practical: build and measure an amplifier and record the measured gain
Values Statement: Courage: students report a measured gain that misses the design value instead of adjusting the numbers.
Source Evidence & Quote
Source Text:
Course: CpE-304 - FUNDAMENTALS OF MIXED SIGNALS AND SENSORS
Program: BSCPE
Year level: 3
Description: This course covers the principles of mixed-signal systems that connect the analog world to digital processors. Topics include analog and digital signals, operational amplifier circuits, signal conditioning and filtering, sampling and quantization, analog-to-digital and digital-to-analog conversion, and the operating principles of common sensors and transducers. Students analyze and design signal-conditioning circuits, interface sensors with microcontrollers and build a small data acquisition system, considering noise, accuracy and calibration throughout.

Verbatim Source Quote (exact): "Course: CpE-304 - FUNDAMENTALS OF MIXED SIGNALS AND SENSORS"
Target Evidence & Quote
Target Text:
Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system and laboratory safety policies; the vision, mission and goals of LCUP and of the department
Week 2: Analog and digital signals: amplitude, frequency, resolution and noise
Week 3: Op-amp circuits: inverting, non-inverting, summing and difference amplifiers
Week 4: Operational amplifier fundamentals: the ideal op-amp model and the characteristics of real op-amps
Week 5: Signal conditioning and active filters
Week 6: EXAMINATION
Week 7: Sampling and quantization: the sampling theorem, aliasing and resolution
Week 8: Analog-to-digital converter architectures: flash, successive approximation and sigma-delta
Week 9: Digital-to-analog converters and their specifications
Week 10: Sensors and transducers: temperature, light, pressure and motion sensors
Week 11: Interfacing sensors with a microcontroller
Week 12: EXAMINATION
Week 13: Data acquisition systems: architecture and design
Week 14: Noise, grounding and shielding in mixed-signal circuits
Week 15: Calibration and measurement accuracy
Week 16: Mixed-signal system design and integration
Week 17: Design project: a sensor-based data acquisition system
Week 18: EXAMINATION

Verbatim Target Quote (exact): "Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system and laboratory"

BSIT-CYBERSECURITY (2025-2026) — CS-301 INTRODUCTION TO DIGITAL FORENSICS (30 review records)

cyber-desc-subtle::description-plan Course-level · description[0].t

Status: DRAFT — developer-written expectation, NOT faculty-reviewed · Review Status: unreviewed · Program/Curriculum: BSIT-CYBERSECURITY (2025-2026) · Course: CS-301 INTRODUCTION TO DIGITAL FORENSICS

Provenance: transcribed_approval_not_confirmed — BSIT-CYBERSECURITY CS-301 curriculum row is transcribed from 2025-2026 registrar image (approval not confirmed offline); uses shared BSIT POs/EGAs without executing program_outcome_sources migration.

Original Instructional Content (Aligned Base)
description[0].t: This course introduces the principles and practice of digital forensics: how digital evidence is identified, acquired, preserved, analysed and reported within the legal and ethical limits of an investigation. Students learn the forensic process and the rules that make evidence admissible; acquire and verify forensic images using write blockers and hash values while keeping a chain of custody; and examine file-system, operating-system, network and memory artifacts to reconstruct events. Laboratory work uses prepared evidence images. The course closes with a simulated incident investigation in which teams examine the evidence, document their procedure and present a truthful forensic report.
Modified Instructional Content (Planted Defect)
description[0].t: This course introduces the foundations of digital forensics, the phases of the forensic process, the legal and ethical rules that govern search authority and the admissibility of electronic evidence, evidence seizure and chain-of-custody documentation, and bit-stream forensic imaging using hardware write blockers and cryptographic hash verification.
CLO Context
CLO 1 [Introductory]: Explain the digital forensic process and the legal and ethical requirements that make digital evidence admissible.
  Mapped POs: PO 1: Articulate and discuss the latest developments in the specific field of practice; | PO 4: Act in recognition of professional, social ethical responsibility;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 2 [Enabling]: Apply sound acquisition, preservation and chain-of-custody procedures to digital evidence using forensic imaging and hash verification.
  Mapped POs: PO 7: Apply computing and other knowledge domains to address real-world problems; | PO 9: Utilize modern computing tools.
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 3 [Demonstrating]: Analyze file-system, operating-system, network and memory artifacts to reconstruct the events of an incident.
  Mapped POs: PO 6: Analyze complex problems, and identify and define the computing requirements needed to design and appropriate solution; | PO 7: Apply computing and other knowledge domains to address real-world problems;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 1 (Critical and Creative thinker): Critical and Creative thinker | EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional

CLO 4 [Integration of Values]: Investigate a simulated incident as a team and report the findings truthfully, respecting the privacy of the people involved.
  Mapped POs: PO 3: Work effectively and independently in multi-disciplinary and multi-cultural teams; | PO 4: Act in recognition of professional, social ethical responsibility;
  Mapped EGAs: EGA 2 (Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional): Competent Catholic Augustinian-Marian Professional | EGA 3 (Socially Responsive Steward): Socially Responsive Steward
ILO & Weekly Lesson Context
18-week plan (15 instructional weeks; exam weeks 6, 12, 18).
Source Evidence & Quote
Source Text:
Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system, laboratory rules and class policies; the vision, mission and goals of LCUP and of CITE
Week 2: Foundations of digital forensics: digital evidence, the forensic process (identification, preservation, collection, examination, analysis and reporting) and the role of the examiner
Week 3: Legal and ethical framework: the Cybercrime Prevention Act, the Data Privacy Act, search authority, the admissibility of electronic evidence and the ethics of the examiner
Week 4: Evidence handling and chain of custody: identification, seizure, labelling, documentation and transport of digital evidence
Week 5: Forensic imaging and hash verification: write blockers, bit-stream images, image formats and MD5 and SHA-256 hash values
Week 7: File systems: FAT, NTFS and ext4 structures, metadata and timestamps
Week 8: Data recovery and file carving: deleted files, slack space and signature-based carving
Week 9: Windows artifacts: the registry, event logs, prefetch files and traces of user activity
Week 10: Timeline analysis: building and interpreting a timeline of an incident
Week 11: Network forensics: packet captures, flow records and the analysis of network traffic
Week 13: Memory forensics: acquiring volatile memory and analyzing processes, network connections and traces of malware
Week 14: E-mail, web browser and cloud artifacts
Week 15: Mobile device forensics: acquisition methods, mobile file systems and application data
Week 16: Anti-forensics and the validation of findings: data hiding, wiping, timestamp tampering and tool validation
Week 17: Forensic reporting and the case presentation: report structure, expert testimony and the simulated incident investigation

Verbatim Source Quote (exact): "Week 1: Course orientation: course objectives, requirements, grading system, laboratory ru"
Target Evidence & Quote
Target Text:
This course introduces the foundations of digital forensics, the phases of the forensic process, the legal and ethical rules that govern search authority and the admissibility of electronic evidence, evidence seizure and chain-of-custody documentation, and bit-stream forensic imaging using hardware write blockers and cryptographic hash verification.

Verbatim Target Quote (exact): "This course introduces the foundations of digital forensics, the phases of the forensic pr"

Evidence Requirement: Description-plan flags PARTIALLY_ALIGNED quoting the omitted artifact-analysis and reporting weeks.

Expected Academic Finding (Developer DRAFT):

[DRAFT — developer-written expectation, NOT faculty-reviewed]
Defect: cyber-desc-subtle (Catalogue 2.2, Component 2, primary, subtle)
Rule: CUR-09 | Relationship: Description-plan (description-plan) | Officially Scored: NO (advisory)
Expected Label(s): PARTIALLY_ALIGNED | Expected Verdict: NEEDS_REVIEW
Developer Rationale: The description covers only Weeks 1 to 5 on the forensic process, legal admissibility, chain of custody and disk imaging, omitting file-system analysis, data recovery, Windows artifacts, timeline construction, network, memory, e-mail and mobile forensics, anti-forensics validation and forensic reporting taught in Weeks 7 to 17.

CLO Context

ILO & Weekly Lesson Context
Week 5 Lesson Context:
ILO 1 [tags: clo:c2]: Acquire a forensic image of a storage device through a write blocker and verify its integrity with hash values.
Weekly Content: Forensic imaging and hash verification: write blockers, bit-stream images, image formats and MD5 and SHA-256 hash values
Activities: - Demonstration of a hardware write blocker and the imaging software installed in the laboratory
- Laboratory: image a prepared USB drive with that software and compare the hash values of the drive and the image
Assessment Tasks: - Five-item multiple-choice quiz on the definition of a cryptographic hash function
Values Statement: Courage: students report a hash mismatch honestly instead of running the tool again until the error disappears.

- supplied_test_materials: Unsupported supplied test materials ("the provided test cases" / "the provided test data") — Should assessment recommendations that rely on faculty-supplied test cases or test data ("the provided test cases", "the given input file") carry an advisory note or withholding code when the lesson does not state that test cases are provided, or are instructor-supplied test inputs an ordinary part of a programming task?
- cross_relationship_copying: Cross-relationship copying in drafts and explanations (copies_other_relationship vs borrowed_wording) — Confirm that any draft or explanation borrowing activities, artifacts, or behaviors from a different lesson week must be rejected or withheld during faculty validation, and verify whether borrowed_wording advisory thresholds match faculty expectations.
